PREFACE 


"Order  of  Battle  of.  the  United  States  Army.,  World  War  II,  Europ- 
ean Theater  of  Operations- -Divisions" was  prepared  in  the  Office  of  the 
Theater  Historian,  Headquarters,  United  States  Forces  European  Theater, 
to  make  available  an  accurate  digest  of  important  facts  concerning  the 
sixty-one  (61)  divisions  assigned  to  the  European  Theater.* 

Presented  in  this  volume  are  command  and  staff  rosters,  compos- 
ition of  divisions,  attachments  and  detachments,  assignments  and  attach- 
ments to  higher  units,  command  post  locations  and  pertinent  statistics. 
With  the  exception  of  the  lists  of  attachments  and  detachments  which 
cover  only  the.  period  of  combat,  the  material  presented  for  each  div- 
ision dates  from,  its  arrival  in  the  European  Theater  to  the  end  of  host- 
ilities on  9  May  1945.  For  units  arriving  from  the  North  African  Theater, 
the  record  begins  on  15  Aug  19--4,  the  date  of  the  invasion  of  Southern 
France.  Units  which  arrived  in  the  European  Theater  after  9  May  1945  are 
not  dealt  with  in  this  volume. 

The  principal  sources  used  were  the,  historical  records  submitted 
by  the  units  involved,  chiefly  the  "After  Action  Reports"  and  support- 
ing documents.  The  research  procedure  for  each  section  of  the  manuscript 
differed,  however;,  and  effort  was  made  to  use  every  document  available 
in  the  files  of  the  Historical  Section.  These  are  specifically  cited  in 
the  explanatory  notes  below.  In  addition,  as  research  on  a  division  was 
completed  as  far  as.  existing  records  permitted,  the  draft  manuscript 
was,  wherever  possible,  submitted  to  the  divi  "ion  for  correction  and 
approval.  A  file  for  each  unit  containing  all  «ork  sheets  and  corre- 
spondence prepared  in  the  course  of  the  research  has  been  retained  and 
forwarded  to  the  War  Department  for  reference. 

The  project  here  presented  was  originally  undertaken  prior  to 
VE  Day  at  the  same  time  that  the  research  and  preparation  of  a  series 
of  narrative  monograph  histories  of  the  war  was  begun. 


#   Hot  included  are: 

1,    Period  of  service  of  1st  Armd  Div  and  34th  Div  in  UK  in 
1942  while  based  there  preparatory  to  invasion  of  North  Africa. 

2-,    Period  of  service  of  1st  Div  in  UK  in  1942  and  in  North 
Africa  and  Mediterranean  areas  in  1942-3. 

3.    All  divisions  in  North  Africa  during  the  period  3  Nov  1942 
(Invasion  date  in  North  Africa)  to  3  Feb  .1943  (when  North  Africa  Thea- 
ter of  Operations  was  established  as  an  independent  command  separates 
from  ETQUSA  ) .  • 


iv 


The  primary  intent  off 1  preparing  an  Order  ;.:4f  Battle  then  was  t»  present 
statistical  raateri-.il  in  convenient  outline''  and  tabular  form,  for  the 
guidance  of  those  onga^ed  in  wore  exhaustive  research.  It  is  fully 
recognized  that  a  definitive  order  ff  battle  ....  «it  await  the  completion 
of  research  much  more  thorough  than' was  possible  under  the  conditions 
laid  dawn  for  this  project,  That  is  presented  here  is  in' essence  a 
•tabulation  of  each  unit's  mwi  official  record,  cross  checked  against 
other  units'  official,  records  and:  in  most  cases  specifically  approved 
by  the  unit is  historical  officer,  'No  claim  is  made  to' any  nigher  truth 
than^a' reproiiuction  of  the  essentials  of  the-  official  record/ as 
accurate  as  circumstances  permitted.-  " 

Although  the  cardinal  consideration  in  the  preparation  of  this 
work  was  accuracy,  it  is  realised  that  many  inaccuracies  still  exist 
in  it.  It  was  shaped  up  against  a  deadline;  in  only  six  months,  in 
contrast  to  the  order  of  battle  ef  "-forld-  '  Jar  I  which,  with  14  fev.er 
divisions  and  59  percent  fewer  combat -.  days,  ms  not  completed  until  12 
'years  after  the  end  of  hostilities.  A' large  part  of  the  research  work 
and  recording  of  facts  was  of  necessity  performed  by  untrained  French 
civilian  personnel;  the  rate  of  turnover  among  thorn  was  high  and  it 
was  never  possible  to  supervise  their  work  in  detail.  Because  the  job 
was  undertaken  during  the.  war  and  finished  in  the  theater  within  a 
few  months  after  cessation,  of  hostilities,  records,  especially  of 
units  rapidly  redeployed  or  demobilized  here,  were  not  always  avaij-v 
labie. 

But  perhaps  the  most  discouraging  source  of  inaccuracy  was 
the  conflicting  evidence  of  various  army  documents  themselves  on  the 
samei'facts .  At  one  period  of  this  study,-  the  historical  section  was 
in  possession  of  three  sets,  of  figures  showing  the  arrival  dates  0f 
divisions  ontthe  continent,  Inch  source  would  normally  be  considered 
a  satisfactory  one.  Agreement  nay  sought  on  165  facts,-  but  -  found  on 
only  75 »  Yet  all  the  sourpes* purported,  to  give,  the  same  fact— the 
arrival  date  of  division  headquarters. 

It  was  soon  recognized  that  the  more  figures  available  the 
more  disagreement  was  invited.  By  the  same  token,  however j  it  was 
realized  that  no  sin  tie  siouroe  could  bo  accepted  as  valid.  The  only 
solution  seemed  to  be  to  seek  by  the  multiplication  of  sources  some 
general  agreement.  This  bvas  usually  found  and  then  a  specific  choice 
was  made  from  the  source  which  critical  evaluation  showed  most  pro- 
bably, reliable. 

Inaccuracy  crept  in  e;guin  in  iiiteridl  submitted  by  divisions 
directly  to  the  Theatep  historian  without  the  check  that  might  have 
been  given  in  the  proeaos  of  forwarding  through  command  channels f 
direct  cori'espon-.-.ence  with  the  divisions  furthermore  was  often  dif- 
ficult and  unsatisfactory,  The  division  historian'  »*<as  frequently 
burdened  with  other  duties  as  the  Division  Public  delations  Officer 
*r  Information  and  E-euc  at  ion  Officer  or  operational  member  of  the

historian  might  arjswer  each  request  for  inf ormation,

With  all  these  provisos,  it  is  probable  that  the.  errors  which  .

COMHAUB  MD  STAFF  LISTS 


•Command  and.  staff,  lists  were,  originally  compiled  in  the  Histor* 
ical  Section  from  the  following  sources j:  ''Rosters  of  Keys  Officers'',  of 
divisions,  corps  and  higher  headquarters  (submitted  monthly  by  the  Ad- 
jutant Genera'l  of  Hq  ETOITSA  to  the  War  Department))  "After  Action  Reports" 
with  supporting  documents*;  and'  units  histories. 

These  sources,  however,  proved  incomplete)  often  lacking  exact 
dates  of  assumption  of  command  and, dates  of  promotion.  Tentative  lists 
were  therefore  sent  to  each  division  historian' for  completion  and  cor*' 
recti  on,:  •  • 

The  first  date  shown  for  each  command  and  staff  position  is  the 
dote 'the  division  headquarters  arrived  in  the  European  Theater  and  does 
not  necessarily  indicate  the  date  the  officer  assumed  the  position,  nor 
does  it  show  the  date  that  particular  officer  arrived  in  the  theater. 
As  an  example,  Up j  Gen  Mwin  Pi  pnrker,  Jr,  assumed  command  of  the  78th 
Infantry  Division  on  15  August  194g,.    However.,  in  this  volume,  the  date- 
shown  opposite  his  name  is  26  October  1944  which  is  the  date  the  78th 
Infantry  Division  headquarters  arrived  in  the  .auroppan  Theater.  . 

In  the  promotion  of  general, officers,  there  are  usually  two 
dates  involved,  the  date  of  the  promotion  order,  and  the  effective  date 
of  rank.    Wherever  possible,  the  date  of  promotion  order  has  been  used. 
However,  where  information,  missing  from  the.  records,  was.  supplied  by 
the  division  through  correspondence  |,t  was  not  always  possible  to  control 
this  factor.  .  ■  ■         '  " 

The  reason  for  selecting  the  date  of  the  order  rather  than  the 
effective  date  of  rank  was  that  it  conforms . more  nearly  to  the  historic 
cal  fact.    For  instance,  on  6  February  1945,  Special  Order  No.  32  Tfar 
Department,  promoted  Brig  Gen  W  G'  Denver,'  Commanding  General  of  the  Qlfti 
Division  to  the  rank  ,of  Major  Ger^era^m  with  an  effective  date  of  rank 
of  5  January  1945,  .  In  this  volume^  the  promotion  is  shown  on  the  latter  d?t 
date,  because  prior  to  6  February  1945,  General  Weaver  was  actually  a 
Brigadier  General^  and  all  historical  documents  will  . rgfer  t©  him  as 
a  Brigadier  General,  .       ■ / 


*  ■    .''After  Action  Reports'',  are  prescribed  by  paragraph  10,  Army  Regular 
tion  345-105  ''Military  Records  f-r  Historical  Records  and  Histories  of 
Organization, "dated  18  Hoy  1929  as  amended.    After  every  battle  or  en- 
gagement- with  the  enemy,  reports  were  mp.de  by  commanders  of  regiments 
separate  battalions  or  squaqrons,  companies  or  detachments  and  by  all  , 
commanders  of  a  higher  grade.    It  was  prescribed  that  such  reports  have 
annexed  thereto  the  unit  and  staff  journals,"  together  with  supporting 
documents,  Hq  STOtJSA  directed  that  these  reports  be  submitted  monthly, 
Upon  receipt  in  Hq  ETGU$A,  these  reports  were  forwarded  to  the  Historic 
cal  Section  for  custody  and  use. 

vii 


Another  reason  for  selecting  .the  date  of  order,  or  later  date, 
is  that  in  some  cases,  a  general  officer  was  reassigned  and  promoted, 
with  an  effective  date  of  rank  which  goes  back  to  the  period  of  his 
former  command,  and,  a  date  of  order  which  applies  to  the  command  to 
which  reassigned.^  Lt  Geri .  L  T .  Gerzrfr  was  relieved  from  command  of  the  V 
.Corps  and,  assigned  to.  the 'coipmand  of  the;  Fifteenth  Army  on  16  January 
1945,  On  that  date    "he  was  .  a'  magor.  general Several  weeks  later' he  was, 
promote^,  to  .'the  rani?,  of . '^eutehant  general*  but  the  effective  date  of 
this  rank  was  retro active" to  the  period  when  he  was  in  command  of  V 
Corps.    However, in  no.  period  of  his. actual  command  of  V  Corps  was  Gene- 
ral. G'.erow  a,,  lieutenant  general. 


ARRIVAL  DAT.-5S  IN  THE  ETO  ON  CONTINENT 


Arrival  dates  in  the  European  Theater  and  arrival  dates  on  the 
continent  were  originally  obtained  from  the  files  of  Central  Machine 
Records  Unit,  Hq  ETOUSAj  "After  -lotion  Reports";  Unit  Histories;  and 
files  of  AG  Operations  Division,'  Hq,- "ETOUSA* 

The  dates  derived,  from  these  sources,  however,  were  often  in  . 
conflict,    All  dates  %-~re  recorded  on  work  sheets  and  sources  of  the 
information  shown,     These  were  checked  against  a  list  of  arrival  dates 
prepared,  by  Hq,-  12th  Army  Group,    But  the  resultant  discrepancies  were 
so  large  that  a  special  request  was  submitted  to  all  divisions  to  fur- 
nish exact  dates.     In  most  cases  the  new  dates  supplied  agreed  with 
those  in  the  ''After  Action  Reports''  and  unit  histories.    As  no  further 
check  could  be  made  short  of  research  beyond  the  scope  of  this  work, 
these  new  figures  were  used.     In  the  case  of  units  which  were  demobi- 
lized or  inactivated  before  they  could  be  .contacted,  the  "After  Action 
Report'*  or  unit  history  dates  were  selected. 

The  arrival  of  a  division,  usually  extended  over  several  days 
and  sometimes  several  months.'  Inclusive  arrival  dates,  however,  would' 
have,  involved  additional  complications.     It  was  'decided  therefore  to 
record  only  the. date  of  arrival  of  the  division  headquarters.  "When 
headquarters  itself  arrived  over  a  period  of  two  or  more  days,  the  one 
date  selected  is  that  on  which  the  main  echelon  closed.    No  attempt 
has  been  made  to  record  the  arrival  of  advanced  detachments,  which 
usually .came  to  the  European  Theater  four  to  six  weeks  before  the 
main  body. 

Since  the  date  shown  is 'the  arrival  date  of  the  main  body  of 
the  division  headquarters ,  there  are  oases  where  a  division  actually 
entered  combat  prior  to  its  stated  arrival  on  the  continent'.  For 
example,  the  three  infantry  regiments  of  the  42d  Infantry  Division 
were  committed  on  24  December  1944,  although  the  division  headquarters 
did  not  arrive  until  18. January  1945,

AWARDS  -AMP  PRORATIONS  ' 

Material  on  awards  and  decorations  was  furnished  by  the  Awards 
Branch, .'"Adjutant  General,  Hq  VSfffi*  A  'monthly  report  was  prepared  by 


that 'Awards  Branch,  based  "Updn  'reports  from 'lower  units,  and  was  for- 
warded to  the 'War  Department",''  1'his.. 'report,  showed  the  number  and.typa.  . 
of  awards^  ■granted  in  each  ..division } corps  and :  army,, /hnd  ^  the  number  of.  ■ 
awards  "given  "  to"  personal :ei;  of  each''  arm 'or"'' service  to'  '30';  September  1945 
inclusive,    The  cumulative  total  of  awards  shown  in  these  monthly  re- 
ports furnished  the  figures  presented,  in  this  volume. 

'"JBhe  figures  of  the'  total'  awards  made  by'  divisions  are  sometimes 
unavoidably  misleading,    For  units 'which  were. still' 'in  the  theater' on 
30  September  1S?45,  this  report  shows  awards  'given" only  up  to  that  .date.. 
For  units  which' departed 'from  the  theater; prior  to  30' September,,  no  ', 
awards  are  shown  after  the  date ''of  departure,  even  though  it  is  known' 
that -swards  based  upc  ..i  combat  "operations  in  tnis  theater  wefe'subse-. 
quently  given  upon  arrival  of  unit  in  the  Zone  of  Interior .    For  exam- 
ple,, the '97th  Inf  Diy  departed  from  the  theater  in.  May  1945,    It  is 
known  that  awards  were  given  in  the  US  based  upon,, European,  combat  ope- 
rations.    Since,  however,  no  report  was/made  to  the  European  Theater,, 
this  information  is  not  in  the'. monthly  thetoter  reports  to  the  War  De- 
partment and  is  not  included  in  this  volume ,  * 

Many  awards,  especially  Bronze  Stars,  were . reported  to  higher 
headquarters  and  incorporated  'in  the  War  Department  monthly  report  but 
were  later  rescinded  by  lower  . unit  orders.    These  rescissions,  were  ca.u-s . 
sed  mostly  by  the  transfer  of  personnel  or  the  discovery  that  the  award, 
had  previously  been  granted.    Because  of  the  complexity  of  recording 
each  rescission  at  higher  headquarters,  the  AG .made  no  attempt  to  alter 
the  original  monthly  figures.    A  small  margin  of  error  should  therefore 
be  figured  in  the  totals  shown  for  Bronze  Stars. 

Figures  on  Purple  Heart  awards  were  not  used;  since  a  large  per- 
centage of  awards,  were  made  in  hospitals,  records  of  which  were  not 
cleared  through  divisions,  complete  data 'was  not  available, 

Records  of  awards  of  Congressional  Medals . of  Honor  and. Distin- 
guished Service  Medals  were  obtained  from  The  Adjutant  General' s  Office 
Machine  Records.  Branch,  from  a  special  report  prepared  on  28.  November 
1945.  'Beceuse  of  their  late  receipt,  they  are.  not  shown  in  the  division 
sion  sections,  and  are  listed  only  in  Table  J  of  pert.. II, 


ATTACHMENTS  AND  DETACHM^TS 

The  information' contained  in  this  section  was  obtained  partly 
through  research  by  the  Historical  Section,  and  partly  from  lists  sub- 
mitted by  divisions.    The  following  .sources,  were  used  by  Historical 
Section  researchers!  "After  Action  Reports";  G-3  periodic  reports  of' 
division  and  corps;  regimental  S-3  periodic  reports; -field  orders,  ines*  • 
sages,  sitrepsj  letters  of  instructions  and  corps  troop  assignment  lists,, 
li  g  ''•  ■ 

Research  w*s  a  long  and  difficult  process,  and  in  many  cases, 
available  records  did  not  furnish  information  upon  which  to  base  a  com- 
plete listing.    Because  of  insufficient  records,  and  lack  of  personnel 
to  complete  the.  *  v3earch  on  all  61  divisions  in  the  time  allotted,  a 


request  was  made  to  divisions  to  submit  this  information.    Some  divi- 
sions replied  that  they  couxd  not  furnish  lists  of  attachments  and  de- 
tachments from  -  available  records..    Sometimes  lists  furnished  by  divi-  ., 
sions  were  so  inadequate  that  an  entirely  new  list  had  to  be,  accompli- 
shed by  Historical  Section  research.  • 

The  principal  means  of  verifying  attachments  and  detachments 
was  the  find  agreement  in  record  between  the  unit,  attached  or  detached 
and  the  unit  to  which  it  was  attached'.    The  volume  of  work,  however, 
involved  in  the  checking  of  each  attachment  in  that  manner  would  have 
precluded  the  completion  of  this  tentative  study_ within ■ the  time  allot- 
ted.   For  this  reason,  only  detachment  and  ..attachment  of  regiments,  com- 
bat commands,  cavalry,  groups  and  organic  divisional  units  were  so  chec- 
ked. 

■    .  Most  discrepancies  found  were  resolved  by  research  of  records 
available  at  the  Historical  Section.    But  since  time  did  not  permit  the 
verification  of  all  attachments  or  detachments  lists  ...submitted  by  divi- 
sions, it  is  to  be  assumed  that  certain  inaccuracies  exist  for  non- 
organic units  of  battalion  strength  .or  smaller. 

Because  records  were  not  available,  no  attempt  was  made  to 
show  attachments  prior  to  a  division' s  entry  into  combat,,  attachments 
shown  which  coincide  with  date  unit  entered  combat  may  have  been  made 
prior,  to  da te.. shown.  .'  .       .  ;  - 

All  cavalry,  units  listed  .in  this  study  are  mechanized . 

When  attachmentsor  detachment  dates  were  not  available,  the 
item  was  left  blank. 


'  '  '     ASSIGNMENT  MB  ATTACHMENT  TO  HIGHER  UNIT 

Assignments  and  attachments  to  higher  units  were  originally 
prepared  from  troop  •assignmenttlists  and  ''After  Action  Reports'1  of  armies 
corps,  and  divisions,  in  addition  to  card  files  maintained  by  AG  Opera- 
tions Division,  Hq  ETOUSA*    The  prepared  lists  were  then  submitted  to 
each  division  for  correction  and  completion.    Upon  receipt  of  corrections 
from  divisions,  lists  were  again  checked  with  originals  compiled  by  the 
Historical  Section. 

Because  certain  records  in  the  historical  section  were  avai- 
lable corroboration  of  attachments  and  assignments,  the  corrections  made 
by  the  division  were  not  accepted  without  further  checking.    Tflaere  the 
division  agreed  with  statements  of  attachment  and  assignmsarttin  the  oriri 
ginal  historical  section  report,  no  further  study  was  made.  Assignments 
were  often  made  verbally,  but  recorded  later'  under  e  different  date  in 
accordance  with  a  written  order..   If  records  of  verbal  orders  were  found 
in  research,  the  date  of  the  verbal  order  was  used  in  this  report.

COMMAND  POST  LISTS 


The  initial  CP  lists  were  prepared  from  the  following  sources: 
12th  Army  Group'!  G-3  (daily)  Reports";  "After  Action  'Reports";  Unit  His- 
tories "(of  which  Only'  six  were  available);  SHAEF"  G-3  War  Room  Daily 
Summaries* jand  '  "Roster  of  Key 'officers",  ■'publiehed*  monthly"  by  Hq  ETOUSA. 

'    One  of  the  primary  difficulties  involved  in  preparing  the  list 
of  ©bmmernd  posts  was  the  inconsistency  of  the  records  in  the  method  of 
locating  them,    SHAEF  G-3  Summaries  listed  them  only  by  map  grid  coori- 
dinutes.    Most  other  sources  indicated' CP  locations'  by  towns.  Twelfth 
Army  Group  Ch$  Reports  listed  grid  coordinates  for  division  CPs,  and 
indicated  higher  headquarters  CPs  on  the  map  accompanying  the  report. 
Units  themselves  generally  referred  to  their  CP  locations  by  relation  to 
the  nearest  town,    In  this  volume,  the  latter  method  for  indicating  all 
division  CPs  was  ado.  ted »  ' 

The  errors  involved  in  handling  and  translating  coordinates  were 
large,  as  was  expected ,    The  tentative  lists  prepared  by  the  Historical 
Section  were  sent  to  divisions  for  check,    In  most  cases  divisions  sub>* 
mitted  entirely  new  lists*  different  both  in  place  names  and  dates,  in 
addition  to  revealing  important  omissions,    In  view  of  this,  only  divls 
sion  approved  lists' were  incorporated  in  this  volume, 

■    Even  the  corrected  CP  lists  submitted  by  the  divisions,  however 
were  inaccurate^  especially  with  regard  to  correct  spelling  of  towns, 
and  additional  checking  was  necessary  before  they  could  be  published.. 
It  Was  obvious  that  many  had  been  carelessly  prepared.    Cities. shown 
were  often  spelled  by  American  phonetics  without  regard  to  the  French 
original. 

Few  divisions  named  the  departments  or  province  of  the  cities 
listed.    The  task  of  identifying  cities  by  departments  and  provinces 
was  completed  in  the  Historical  Section,    Those  cities  which  were  ob- 
viously misspelled  were  corrected  by.  a  careful  study  of  the  division' s 
route  of  advance.    Some  of  this  had  to  be  informed  guesswork,  and  some 
errors  may  therefore  remain. 

The  problem  of  determining  the  correct  geographical  subdivision, 
made  necessary  by  the  multiplication  of  the  same  place  name  in  a  single 
country,  was  further  complicated  by  the  fact  that  many  French  cities 
have  compound  names  but  are  generally  referred  to  in  their  own  locality 
by  the  first  name  only.    When  divisions  neglected  to  give  the  complete 
name  of  the  city ,  there  was  sometimes  a  possibility  of  locating  it  in 
the  wrong  department,    The  most  difficult  case  was  St,  Aubin.  France., 
There  are  sixteen  (16)  cities  nomed  St,  Aubin,  plus  sixtyone  (61)  with 
names  including  St»  Aubin,  like  St,  Aubin  Celleville^  St  Aubin  de  Cretor, 
and  St.  Aubin  sur  Yonno. 

The  towns  on  the  French  border  in  the  departments  of  Moselle, 
Bas-rRhin,  and  Haut-rRhin  were  sometimes  given  in  German  form  and  some- 


times  in  French.    This  presented  another  problem  in  identifying 
the  cities  with  the  proper  department* 

;  "In  order  to :  Identify' towns,- without  ambiguity," the  counties 
were  named -.for  citi.es.  in  the  United,  Kingdom;  provinces  for  cities 
in  the  Netherlands, ,  B  elgium,"AuStria  ;.and  ■  Czechoslovakia";  ■  Depart- 
ments for  French  cities;  and  administrative  divisions  were  shown 
for  German  towns.     The 'Grand  Luchy  Of .  Luxembourg  has  no' political 
subdivisions,  equivalent  in  size  to. provinces  or  counties* 

In  Germany,  where  ..older  geographical  divisions  were  ovei~-  . 
■  laid  by  "ijiazi,  party  'administrative  divisions,  ~the  nomenclature 
as  -  established  by  the  Third  Reich  ."was  . used. 

In  cases  where  the  Anglicized . spelling  of  foreign  cities 
or  political  subdivisions  is  more  easily. recognized  by  the 
general  reader  than  is  the  foreign  spelling,  the  Anglicized  vers- 
ion has  been  used.     For.  example,  .''iAjnich'*  has  been ,  used •  i-n  place 
■of  "iaunchen"  and  ^Bavaria*1  in  place  of  "Bayern".  ^'Otherwise  the 
spelling  used  is  that  recognized  by  the  country-  involved, 

. .  i  It  will  be  noted  that  the  date  shown  for  the  initial  Com- 
mand fpst  Location  doss  not  usually  agree  with. the  date  shown 
for  the  arrival  of  the  division  in  the  theater  or  the  first  date 

...shown  for.  each  Gouvfuand  and.  Staff  officer,   j^w^-may  bef  explained 
by  the  fact  that  some  division^  reported --CPs-  which  were  set  up 

.in  advance  of  the  arrival  of .the  main  body  of  division 'headquart- 

..ersj  whereas.,  other  divisions:  did  not  report  a  <3P  'until  the  main 

-  body  hod  actually  arrived  at  the  initial  CP.  .:

2d    INFANTRY  DIVISION 


''Indianhead  Division'1 


The  division  insignia  consists 
of  an  Indianhead  on  a  white  star 
superimposed  upon  a  black  shield. 
According  to  available  informa- 
tion, the  insignia  is  the  crea- 
tion of  a  truck  driver  of  the 
division,  who  painted  the  design 
on  the  side  of  his  truck  during 
Yforld  War  I. 

kiotto:     '^Second  To  lone".

k  3£    INPANTRY. DIVISION 


"Ivy  Division** 


Inherited  from  "the  original  4th 
Division  of  World  War  J,  the  in- 
signia is  composed  of  four  green 
ivy  leaves  attached  at  the  stems 
and  opening  at  the  four  corners 
of  a  square  on  a  brown  back- 
ground. 'Jhe  word  "i-vy",  as  pro- 
nounced, suggests  the  characters 
used  in  the  Soman  numeral 

Kpttoj    "Steadfast  and  loyal".

1  i£    I  I  P  A  g  T  R  Y  '  DIVISION 


''Red,  Diamond  Division" 


It  is  the  diamond  that  cut  into 
the  line  of  the  /yosgss  in  August 
1918,  ana  by  the  capture  of  yra- 
pelle  made  the  only  indentation 
suffered  by  the  Germans  in  their 
southern  sectors  in  three  years 
of  trench  warf are ;  that  helped 
shear  off  the  salient  of  St. 
Mihiel;  and  that  after  slowly 
grinuing  the  Boche  cut  of  the 
Bois  des  Rappes, became  the  point 
of  an  arrow  that  pierced  the 
Mouse  and  thereby  gave  the  divi- 
sion its  name.. 

Motto;    "We  will"

'^Yankee  Division ,? 


During  World  Wrr  I,  a  press  con- 
ference of  Boston  .  newspapermen 
Was  or  11'  d  by  the  Commanding 
General  to  determine  a  nickname 
for  this  division ,  which  had 
just  be.en  inducted  from  New  Eng- 
land National  Guard  units.  The 
adopted  suggestion  wcs,  "Call  it 
the  'Yankee  Division1  as  all  New- 
Jsng  landers  are  Yankees ?nd  a 
dark  blue,  acrogrnmmed  '  YD  '  on 
an,  olive  drab  background  was  off 
ficially  designated  as  the  divi- 
sion insignia.

-  108 


2  8  th     INFANTRY  DIVISION 


"Bloody  Bucket  Division" 
"Key stone  Division" 


The  red  keystone,  official  em- 
blem of  the  State  of  Pennsylva- 
nia, is  the  official  shoulder 
sleeve  .  insignia  of  the  28th  Di- 
vision which  was  originally  a 
Pennsylvania  National  Guard  or- 
ganization. The  Germans  called 
it  the  "Bloody  Bucket"  because 
of  the  blood-red  keystone  insig- 
nia and  vicious  fighting  tactics 
during  the  Normandy  Campaign, 

Motto;     "Fire    and  Movement".

"Blue  and  Gray  Division" 


The  division  insignia,  as  sug- 
gested by  Major  J»A»  Ulio  (later 
llejor  General,  the  Adjutant  C-en-r 
eral),  then  Division  Adjutant,  is 
the  monad,  the  Korean  sjrmbol  of 
eternal  life,  It  is  half  blue 
and  half  grey  in  matching  tear- 
drop design.  The  colors  represent 
the  tradition  of  the  division, 
composed  of  men  of  both  North 
and  South,  whose  forefathers 
f aught  in  the  Union  (blue)  and 
the  Confederate  (gray)  Armies 
.  during  the  Civil  War.

Jackson,  Tennessee  statesman  who 
led  troops  from  Tenn?ssee  and 
the  Garolinas  in  ti.  s  Viar  of  1812. 
When  this,  insignia  Tias  first 
icsued  in  France  in  1918,  it  was 
inadvertently  won?  with  the  *0" 
on  it j  cide,  but  was  changed  in 
1940  t?  an  upright  position*  The 
insignia  combines  the  initials 
"O*  and  "-h"  end  the  Eoaoi  turner  al 
"XXX"  ir.  blue,  with  an  oval-shaped 
background  in  red*

1  1  jl1!    INFANTRY  DIVISION 


"Sante  Fe  Division" 


The  35th  Division  insignia  is  a 
white  cross  "within  a  white  wagon 
wheel  on  a  blue  field.  Built  a- 
round  a  nucleus  of  Nebraska, Kan^ 
sas  and  Missouri  National  Guard 
units,  the  division  trained  for 
World  War  I  in  the  vicinity  of 
the  old  Sante  Fe  trail,  and 
therefore  adopted  the  insignia 
which  represents  the  Sante  Fe 
Cross  f

36  tli  INFANTRY  DIVISION 


'*Lone  Star  Division" 
"Panther  Division" 


The  30th  Division  insignia  con- 
sisting of  an  olive  drab  "T"  on 
a  blue  flint  arrowhead  was  a- 
uo.) a  in  1918.  In  World  War  I, 
the  division  was  organised  from 
National  Guard  units  of  Oklahoma 
and  Taxes;  the  flint  arrowhead 
represents  the  State  of  Oklahoma 
(once  the  Indian  Territory),  and 
the  "I"  is'  for  Texas.

"Rainbow  Divi  s i on " 


In  1917  men  were  selected  from 
various  units  of  the  K&tional 
Guard  of  26  states  and  the  Dis- 
trict of  Columbia  to  form  a 
truly  representative  aiiirisian 
with  a  view  towards  "bringing 
the  war  home'"  to  the  people.  It 
was  numerically  designated  the 
4 2d  Division,  and  called  the 
"Rainbow  Division''  by  General 
Dougl  c Arthur,  then  Colonel 
and  ,  newly  appointed  Chief  of 
Staff  of  the  Division,  because 
"it  spanned  the  nation  like  a 
rainbow" .So  further  characterize 
the  nickname,  the  adopted  Divi- 
sion insignia  resembles  a  rain- 
bow in  bright, red, gold,  and  blue 
colors .

k  k  $h    II?  AMB  Y  DIVISION 


The  division  insignia  consists 
of  two  "blue  figure  4's  back  to 
back  on  a  golden  r  range  circular 
background  within  a  blue  border. 
The  colors  are  those  of  the  .house 
of  Hassau,  whose  Dutch  settlers 
inhabited  what  'is  now  New  York 
and  Hew  Jersey,  and  are  used 
because  the  division  was  organ- 
ized from  Hational  Guard  units 
of  this  region.

"Thunderbird  Division''' 


The  division  insignia  is  a  golden 
thunderbird  on  a  red  square.  The 
thunderbird  is  a  symbol  of  the 
Southwestern  Indians  meaning  srt 
cred  bearer  of  unlimited  happi- 
ness.The  Spanish  colors,  red  and 
gold,  indicate  that  the  four 
States  (Colorado,  Olclshona,  Hew 
Mexico,  and  Arizona)  represented 
by  National  Guard  units  in  the 
division  were  settled  by  the 
Spaniards. Bach  side,  of  the  squaws:- 
represents  a  StateT

6^"a    INFANTRY  DIVISION 


'♦Blood  and  Fire  Division'' 


The  division  insignia  is  a  blood* 
tipped  golden  sword  on  a  sheet 
of  crimson  flame.  The  Commanding 
General,  Major  General  L^SJ.Hibbs, 
inspired  by  the  promise  to  the 
enemy  df  president  Rooseyelt  and 
Prime  Minister  Churchill  at  Casa-^ 
blanca  In  1943  "To  bleed  and 
burn  in  expiation  of  th«-ir  crimes 
against  hmvipnity"|  developed  tiiis 
emblemtA  card  showing. the  emblem 
explaining  its  significance*  and 
containing  ths  signatures  of  10 
civil  and  military  leaders  of 
Great  Britain  and  the  United 
States  vv" ho  were  participants  at 
the  Casablanca  Conference,  has 
been  placed  in  Viar  Department 
archives.

-6.  1  ttL    I  N  £  A  N  T  H  Y  DIVISION 


The  65th  Infantry  Division,  shoul- 
der patch  is  a  white  haibert  on 
a  blue  shi « la.  The  halbertj  a 
sharp  pointed  battle*  ax,  was  a 
potent  weapon  of  the  15th  Cm* 
tury  foot  soldi  f.r,  being  suitable 
either  for  a  powerful  cutting 
smash  or  for  a  quick  thrust*.  It 
was  selected  by  Major  General 
SfpU  Reinhart,U.oi'Drnanding  General 
of  the  (55th,  as  an  emblem  that 
would,  signify  both  the  shock 
action  and  the  speed  of  the 
modern' infantry  division.

6  6  til    I  H  P  A  IT  T  R  Y  DIVISION 


The  division  insignia  is  a  black 
panther's  head,  on  a  .circular 
orange  background  within  a  red. 
border,  The  black  panther  was 
chosen'  to  symbolize  the  attri- 
butes of  a  good  infantryman: 
ability  to  kill,  to  be  aggressra% 
alert,  stealthy,  cunning,  agile, 
and  strong.

1  9.  M    I  M  I  A  K  T'JR-Y  DIVISION 


^Irailhlazers" 


The  division  insignia  consists 
of  a  white  ax,  a  snowy  mountain, 
and  a  gre^n  fir  tree  on  a  red 
background*  The  white  ax  Is  in 
recognition  of  the  pioneers  who 
travelled  the  Oregon  Trail  to 
the  Willamette  Valley,  site  of 
Camp  Adair  where  the  greater 
part  of  the  division's  training 
was  accomplished j the  snowy  movm* 
tain  represents  Oregon's  iilt.Hoodj 
and  the  green,  fir  tree  finds  its 
origin  in  the  insignia  of  the 
91st  Division, ..  from  which  the 
cadre  of  the  70th  Division  was 
drawn*

l^k  ill    I  E  Z  A  N  T  R .  Y  DIVISION 


"Onaway  Division" 


The  division  insignia  is  an  es- 
cutcheon which  has  a.  red  field 
and  p  "blue  chief,  s operated  by 
an  olive  drab  line;  a  three- 
pronged  white  device  is  superior 
posed  on  the  blue  chief.  The 
white  device  was  a  medieval  Eng- 
lish heraldic  symbol  meaning 
"first  son".  In.  World.  War  1  the 
76th  was  the  first  division  of 
the  National  Army  to  be  drawn 
from  civilian  ranks  through  the 
draft,  hence  the  insignia  end 
the  description,  "the  first  sons 
of  the  nation", The  combined  col- 
ors of  the  field,  the  device,  and 
the  chief  are  the  national  col- 
ors, "On away*  is  the  alert  cry 
of  the  Chippewa  Indians,  in  whose 
hunting  grounds  the  76th  Division 
trained.

1  1         I  II  AUK  Y  PIIISION 


^Cross  of  Lorraine  Division '* 


The  insignia  of  the  79th  Divi- 
sion is  a  gray  Lorraine  cross  on 
a  blue  shield  with  a  gray  border? 
it  was  adopted  during  Yforld  War 
I,  Having  distinguished  itself 
at  liontfaucon,  in  Lorraine,  the 
division  selected  the  Gross  of 
Lorraine,  a  symbol  of  triumph 
since  the  15th  Century,  as  its 
insignia.

8  0  th    INFANTRY      D  I  V  I  S  ION 


"Blue  Ridge  Division" 


The  division  insignia  is  a  white 
bordered  escutcheon  of  gold  em-4 
blazoned  with  three  azure  blue 
mountain  peaks* It  symbolizes  the 
three  "Blue  Ridge"  States,  Penn- 
sylvania, Virginia  and  West  Vir- 
ginia, from- which  the  majority 
of  the  80th' s  World  War  I  person- 
nel were  drawn.

3  2    d    AIRBORNE  DIVISION 


"All  American-  Division u 


The  division  ' insignia  consists 
of  the  letters  "M"  inscribed  in 
wiiite  on  a  blue  circle,  i\rhich  in 
turn  is  superimposed  on  a  bright 
red  square  background.  The  82d 
Infantry  Division  in  ;jorld  War  I 
was  comprised  of  soldiers  from 
every  state  in  the  Union  and 
therefore f  the  nickname  "All 
American"  was  adopted  and  "AA" 
was  included  in  the  insignia,, 
liken  in  1942  the  82d  was  react- 
ivated and  made  airborne,  the 
word  "Airborne",  in  white  let- 
ters, was  placed  on  a  crescent-T- 
shaped  blue  background,  and  add- 
ea  to  the  top  of    the  insignia.

''Thunderbolt  Division" 
"Ohio  Division" 


The  division  insignia  consists 
of  a  monogr  aromatic  design  in 
gold  . spelling  put  the  word  ,f0-E- 
I*•0',  on  a  black  triangular  back- 
ground. The  insignia  was.  selec- 
ted during  World  War  J  because 
the  division  contained  mostly 
Ohio  draftees,  *,v en  though  the 
division  changed  from  an  Ohio 
outfit  to  one  representing  the 
48  States/  the  insignia  still 
remains;  however,  a  new  nickname 
'♦Thunderbolt",  was  selected  while 
the  division  was  holding  on  the 
Rhine  opposite  Dusseldorf*

"Railsplitter  Division" 
"Lincoln  Division*'  . 


During  World  War  I  the  84th 
Division  was  referred  to  as  the 
"Lincoln'1  division  because  it 
was  primarily  made  up  of  Nation- 
al Guard  'units,  frora  Illinois* 
Kentucky f .  and  Indi&ne- — the  Lin- 
coln states.  Its  original  in* 
signia  was  a  red  ax  on  a  white 
background  within  a  red  circle, 
with  the  name  ''Lincoln'?  above 
the  ax  and  the  number  ^84"  below 
it» .  The  present  insignia  con- 
sists of  a  white  ax  splitting  a 
white  rail  on  a  red  circular 
background.  Both  insignias  recall 
President  Lincoln's  youthful  use 
of  the  aXf.  Tfith  the  addition 
of  a^  split  rail,  the  division 
adopted  the  new  nickname  of 
'    "Rail  splitters*?.

"Blackhawk  Division." 


The  division  insignia  consists 
of  a  small  red  shield  with  the 
initials  "B'f  and  "H"  in  black 
superimposed  upon  a  design  of  a 
blackhawk  which,  in  turn,  is 
superimposed  upon  a  red  shield. 
The  personnel  of  the  division 
was  originally  drawn  from  the 
states  of  Illinois  •$  Wisconsin, 
and  Minnesota,  foraerly  the  ter- 
ritory inhabited  by  Oh Black 
Hawk  snci  his  tribe.  The  insignia 
is  a  tribute  to  the  pioneers  of 
this  sector,  and  in  recognition 
of  their  prowess  in  battles  with 
the  Jnuiaas*  The  bird  symbolizes 
keenness,  cunning,  and  tenacity.

-  324  - 


3  9    th    INFANTRY  DIVISION 


"Middle  Wpjrt.  Division" 

"Roiling  4  Division" 


The  division  insignia  consists 
of  a  black  within  a  circular 
black  border.  The  division  per- 
sonnel, during,  jforld  War  I,  was 
drawn  primarily  from  the  Middle 
West,  and  the  "  J"  indicates  its 
western  origin;  when,  inverted, 
the  letter  becomes  "M"  anddthus 
the  name,  "Middle  Jest'1  Divi- 
sion. On  its  side,  the  letter 
represents  the  Greek  letter 
"sigma",  the  symbol  of  summation 
that  is,  coordination  or  single 
effort.  The  circle  is  that 
geometrical  figure  which  can 
come  to  stability  in  any  posi- 
tion, yet  move  rapidly  in  answer 
to  a  change  of  forces. 

Motto: "Get    It  Done!"

th    INFANTRY  DIVISION 


The  division  insignia  is  a* cir- 
cular design  consisting  of  a 
black  "9"  on  a  silver  background 
and  a  silver  "4"  on  a  black  back- 
ground separated,  by  a  diagonal 
line,  within  an  olive  drab  bor- 
der. The  insignia  has  no  heral- 
dic significance.

"Victory  Division" 


The  division  insignia,  oonsistjs 
of  a  rnanograEimatic  red  "9"  and 
a  white  Roman  ffV"  on  a  blue 
elliptical  background.  The  Soman 
"Vlsignifi es  the  division  nick- 
name, "Victory  Division"*  and 
the  combination  of  the  Arabic 
"9''  and  the  Roman  represent 
the  division's  number.  The  red 
white,  end  blue  colors  parallel 
the  colors  of  the  iimerican  flag*

9  9    th    INFANTRY  DIVISION 


The  division  insignia  is  a  three 
lined  checkerboard  design  of 
blue  and  white  squares  centered 
on  a  black  shield.  Originally 
planned  as  a  Pennsylvania  "...unit , 
the  horizontal  band  of  white  ...and 
blue  squares  was  adopted  'f^csm 
the  coat  of  anus  of  >;illiam  Pitt 
for  whom  the  city  of  Pittsburgh 
wag  named.  The  black  background 
represents  the  iron  district  of 
Pennsylvania.

10  1  st    AIRBORNE  DIVISION 


"Eagle  pi  vision." 


The  division  insignia  is  a  white 
eagle's  head  with  a  gold  "beak 
on  a  black  shield.  The  design 
is  based,  on  an  African  Civil 
War  tradition.  The  black  shield 
recalls  the  "Iron  Brigade",  one 
of  the  forerunners  of  'tits  101»t 
Division.  One  regiment  '  of  the 
brigade  possessed  th©  famous  war 
eagle,  "Old  Abe",  pictured  on 
the  shield, that  went  through  36 
Rattles  as  a  fierce,  screaming 
mascot  and  was  wounded  twice. 
,;hen  the  division  was  activated 
in  1942,  the  word  "Airborne" , in 
gold  letters,  was  placed  on  a 
crescent-shaped  black  background 
and  added  to  the  top  of  the  in- 
s  ignia ,

I  o  1    .4    I  K  JF  A  N  T  R  y  DIVISION 


"Ozark  Division" 


The  division  insignia  combines 
a  golden  "0",  "Z'U  end  an  a*"e  on 
a  circular  blue  background  to 
spell  out  the  nickname  of  the 
division,  "Ozark" t  In  1921,  the 
division  became  part  of  the  Or- 
ganized Reserve  and  was  allocated 
to  the  states  of  Arkansas  and 
Missouri,  the  Ozark  region,  and 
therefore  adopted  the  design. 
When  the  French  settled  in  this 
area,  they  found  it  inhabited  by- 
Indians  who  were  very  proficient 
with  the  bov;  and  arrow,  and  they 
therefore  celled  the  entire  re- 
gion "Terre  aux  arcs"  or  "Bow 
Country".  "Ozark"  is.  an  Ameri- 
canization of  "aux  arcs",  and 
the  arc  in  the  insignir?  actually 
represents  an  Indian  bow  and 
signifies  marksmanship.  Gold  and 
blue  are  the  colors  which  tra- 
dition has  always  associated  witi 
valor  and  distinction. 

Motto ! 

"Distinction,  Valor,  Marksmanship11

123    d    INFANTRY  DIVISION 


"Cactus  Division" 


The  division  insignia  consists 
of  a  yellow  disc  with  a  green 
oaguaro  cactus  superimposed  upon 
a  patch  of  blue,  and  was  adopted 
in  1922  when  this  reserve  diyi*- 
sion  had  its  headquarters  in  Den- 
ver, Colorado.  'The  yellow  disc 
represents  a  golden  sky,  while 
the  green  cactus  growing  out  of 
the  blue  sage-covered  earth 
characterizes  the  southwest.

2  ^ARMORED  .DIVISION 


"Hell  On  Wheels" 


The  mixture  of  insignia  and  dis* 
tinctive  colors  of  several  arms 
incorporated  in  tfte  Armored  Force 
symbolize  integrity  end  esprit. It 
is  an  interlocked  ornament,  found 
in  Hordio  monuments j  composed  of 
three  torques t  red  for  Artillery} 
blue  for  Infsntryj  and  yellow  for 
Cavalry.  The  symbols  represent 
the  characteristics  of  .-Armored 
Divisions:  the  tank  track,  mo- 
bility and  armor  protection;  the 
cannon*  fire  power j  and  the  red 
bolt  of  lightning,  shock,  action.

"Spearhead'' 


The  mixture  of  insignia  and  disr 
tir.ctiv.-j  colors  of  several  arms 
incorporated  in  the  Armored  Force 
symbolize  integrity  and  esprit, It 
■  is  an  interlocked  or&ounent,  found 
in  Nordic  monuments,  composed  of 
three  torques i  red  for  Artilleryj- 
blue  for  Infantry j  and  yellow  for 
Cavalry*  The  symbols  represent 
the  characteristics  of.  Armored 
Divisions:  the  tank  track,  mo^ 
bility  and  armor  protection!  the 
cannon,  fire  power;  and  the  red 
bolt  of  lightning,  shock  action.

•  U  til    ARMORED  DIVISION 


The  mixture  of  insignia  ancL.dis*»~ 
tinctive  colors  of  several  arms 
incorporated  in  the  Armored  Force 
symbolize  integrity,  and  esprit, It 
is  an  interlocked  ornament, found 
in  Mbrdio  monuments,  composed  of 
three  torques j  red  for  Artillery; 
blu'e  fdr  Infantry;  and  yellow  for 
Cavalry.  The  .symbols  represent 
the  characteristics  of  Armored 
Divisions;  The  tank  tracki  mo* 
bility  and  armor  protection}  the 
cannon,  fire  pov^erj  and  the  red 
bolt  of  lightning,  sho<?k  action.

1  .I1}    ARM  0  RE  -D'  DIVISION 


"Victory" 


The.  mixture  .  of  insignia  and  dis- 
tinctive colors  of  several  arms 
incorporated  in  the  Armored  Force 
symbolize  integrity  end  esprit. It 
is  an  interlocked  ornament, found 
in  liordic  monuments,  composed  of 
three  torques;  red  for  Artillery; 
blue  for  infantry;  and  yellow  for 
Cavalry,  The  symbols  represent 
the  characteristics  of  'Armored 
Divisions;  the  tank  track,  mo- 
bility and  armor  protection;  the 
cannon j  fire  power;  and  the  red 
bolt  pf  lightning,  shock  action.

"Super  Sixth" 


The  mixture  of  insignia  and  dis- 
tinctive colors  of  several  arms 
incorporated  in  the  Armored  Force 
symbolize  integrity  and  esprit. It 
is  an  interlocked  ornament , found 
in  Nordic  monuments composed  of 
three  torques;  red  for  Artillery; 
blue  for  Infgntryj  and  yellow  for 
Cavfiryr  The  symbols  represent 
the  characteristics  of  Armored 
Divisions-  the  tank  track,  mo- 
bility end  armor  protection ;  the 
cannon,,  fire  power;  and  the  red 
bolt  of  lightning,  shock  action.

8  th    ARMORED  DIVISION 


ihund er ing  Herd " 


The  mixture  of  insignia  and  dis- 
tinctive colors  of  several  arms 
incorporated  in  the  Armored  Force 
symbolize  integrity  and  esprit»It 
is  an  interlocked  ornament, found 
in  Nordic  monuments,  composed  of 
three  torques:  red  for  Artillery; 
blue  for  Infantry;  end  yellow  for 
Cavalry.  The  symbols  represent 
the  characteristics  of  Armored 
Divisions:  the  tank  track,  mo- 
bility and  armor  protection;  the 
cannon,  fire  pew or j  and  the  red 
bolt  of  lightning,  shock  action,

1  0  th    A  R  M  0  R  E  D  DIVISION 


"Tiger'1 


The  mixture  of  insignia  and  dis- 
tinctive colors  of  several  arms 
incorporated  in  the  Armored  Force 
symbolise  integrity  and  tsprit.lt 
4s  an  interlocked  ornament, found 
in  Nordic  monuments,  composed  of 
three  torques;  red  for  Artillery; 
blue  for  Infantry;  and  yellow  for 
Cavalry.  The  symbols  '  represent 
the  characteristics  of  Armored 
Divisions:  the  tank  track,  mo- 
bility and  armor  protection;  the 
cannon,  fire  power;  and  the  red 
b»lt  of  lightning, • shock  action.

iikM    ARMOEBD  DIVISION 


+ 

The  mixture  of  insignia  and  disr 
tinctive  colors  of  several  arms 
incorporated  in  the  Armored  Force 
symbolize  integrity  and  esprit, Jt 
is  an  interlocked  ornament,  |"ound 
in  Bordic  monuments,  composed  of 
three  torques i  red  for  Artillery^ 
blue  for  infantry;  and  yellow- for 
Cavalry.  The  symbols  represent 
the  characteristics  of  Armored 
Pivisions;  the  tank  tracks  mo-* 
bility  and  armor  protection;  the 
cannon,  fire  power;  and  the  red 
bolt  of  lightning,  shock  action*

-  545  - 


16  th    ARMORED  DIVISION 


The  mixture  of  insignia  and  dis- 
tinctive colors  of  several  arms 
incorporated  in  the  Armored  Force 
symbolize  integrity  and  esprit, It 
is  an  interlocked  ornament, found 
in  Nordic  monuments,  composed  of 
three  torques!  r:ed  for  Artillery; 
blue  for  Infantry;  and  yellow  for 
Cavalry.  The  symbols  represent 
the  characteristics  of  Armored 
Divisions;  the  tank  track,  mo- 
bility and  armor  protection;  the 
cannon,  fire  power;  and  the  red 
bolt  of  lightning,  shock  action.

20th    ARMORED  DIVISION 


The  mixture  of  insignia  and.  dis- 
tinctive colors  of  several  arms 
incorporated  in  the  Armored  Force 
symbolize  integrity  and.  esprit. It 
is  an  interlocked  ornament, found 
in  Nordic  monuments,  composed  of 
three  .torques*  red  for  Artillery; 
blue  for  Infantry;  and  yellow  for 
Cavalry.  The  symbols  represent 
the  characteristics  of  Armored 
Divisions:  the  tank  track,  mo* 
bility  and  armor  protection;  the 
cannon^  fire  power;  and  the  red 
bolt  of  lightning,  shock  action,
